People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

But it does seem like that’s when it started building—around when I took over the STEM-Arts curriculum initiative, about eight months ago. At first I kept telling myself it was just being busy, but it’s sort of stayed with me.\n\nDoctor: If the curriculum initiative were no longer on your plate, do you think you would still feel this way?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not completely sure, but I think some of it would still be there. I’d like to say it’s just the initiative, because that would make sense, wouldn’t it? But even when I’m not actively dealing with it, most days there’s still this heavy gray feeling over everything. I can be in the classroom or at home with my husband and still feel kind of hollow… close to tears, even when nothing specific is happening.\n\nDoctor: Before these past eight months, was there ever a period of at least two months when your mood felt mostly back to normal?\n\nPatient: Before that, yes, I think my mood was mostly normal. I mean, I had ordinary stress, like anyone, but this particular feeling has really just been building for about eight months, since I took over the STEM-Arts curriculum initiative. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about a lot of different things?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say “a lot of different things,” exactly. I mean, I worry, probably more than I should sometimes, but it’s mostly around the school responsibilities and whether I’m keeping up. I’m not sure if that counts as excessive… does it?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your sleep?\n\nPatient: Yes… my sleep hasn’t been great. I mean, I can still function, but it’s been pretty all over the place for months. I’ll lie there replaying grant details or things I need to fix, and then a lot of nights I wake up around three or four and can’t really settle again. Even if I do sleep longer, I wake up feeling like I didn’t actually rest. Is that the kind of change you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually tired or low on energy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I do get tired, but I’ve been telling myself that’s probably just normal with work and not sleeping perfectly. I wouldn’t want to overstate it. Some days I feel a bit drained, but I can still get through what I need to do. Does that answer it?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

I wouldn’t want to overstate it. Some days I feel a bit drained, but I can still get through what I need to do. But most days it does feel like there’s this heavy gray film over everything, and I can be in class with students or sitting at home with my husband and still feel… hollow, I guess. Like I’m close to tears for no clear reason. Is that what you mean by hopeless?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling especially worthless or guilty lately?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes, a bit. I don’t want to sound dramatic, because I know everyone makes mistakes, but when anything goes wrong with the curriculum project—even something small—I start thinking I’m letting the students down. And then I get stuck on this idea that I’m wasting what my parents sacrificed for me to have opportunities like this. I know that may not be totally fair, but it feels pretty real in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I haven’t noticed anything that feels dramatically different. Sometimes I have to reread something or refocus myself, but I think that’s fairly normal, isn’t it? I can still follow things for the most part.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that life isn’t worth living, or thoughts of wanting to die?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t want you to think I’m being unsafe or anything. I haven’t made a plan. But yes, sometimes, especially late at night when I’m still in the studio, I catch myself thinking it would be a relief if I just… didn’t wake up.\n\nThat scares me to say. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I just haven’t been feeling like myself lately. That’s probably what finally made me think I should come in, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but most days it feels like there’s this heavy gray film over everything. I can be in the classroom with the kids, or at home with my husband, and still feel kind of hollow… like I’m close to tears for no clear reason. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… maybe about eight months, I think. It sort of started building after I took over the STEM-Arts curriculum initiative. I kept telling myself it was just stress from that, you know, like anyone would feel stretched, but it hasn’t really lifted.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel this started because of the curriculum initiative taking over your life?\n\nPatient: I think it probably contributed, yes. I don’t want to blame it entirely, because I know plenty of people handle big projects and don’t… fall apart or anything. But it does seem like that’s when it started building—around when I took over the STEM-Arts curriculum initiative, about eight months ago. At first I kept telling myself it was just being busy, but it’s sort of stayed with me.\n\nDoctor: If the curriculum initiative were no longer on your plate, do you think you would still feel this way?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not completely sure, but I think some of it would still be there. I’d like to say it’s just the initiative, because that would make sense, wouldn’t it? But even when I’m not actively dealing with it, most days there’s still this heavy gray feeling over everything. I can be in the classroom or at home with my husband and still feel kind of hollow… close to tears, even when nothing specific is happening.\n\nDoctor: Before these past eight months, was there ever a period of at least two months when your mood felt mostly back to normal?\n\nPatient: Before that, yes, I think my mood was mostly normal. I mean, I had ordinary stress, like anyone, but this particular feeling has really just been building for about eight months, since I took over the STEM-Arts curriculum initiative. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about a lot of different things?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say “a lot of different things,” exactly. I mean, I worry, probably more than I should sometimes, but it’s mostly around the school responsibilities and whether I’m keeping up. I’m not sure if that counts as excessive… does it?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your sleep?\n\nPatient: Yes… my sleep hasn’t been great. I mean, I can still function, but it’s been pretty all over the place for months. I’ll lie there replaying grant details or things I need to fix, and then a lot of nights I wake up around three or four and can’t really settle again. Even if I do sleep longer, I wake up feeling like I didn’t actually rest. Is that the kind of change you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually tired or low on energy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I do get tired, but I’ve been telling myself that’s probably just normal with work and not sleeping perfectly. I wouldn’t want to overstate it. Some days I feel a bit drained, but I can still get through what I need to do. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling hopeless?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if hopeless is the right word. I try not to think of it that way. It scared me enough that I told my husband I couldn’t keep handling those thoughts alone. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your appetite?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a way I’ve really paid attention to. That’s probably what finally made me think I should come in, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but most days it feels like there’s this heavy gray film over everything. I can be in the classroom with the kids, or at home with my husband, and still feel kind of hollow… like I’m close to tears for no clear reason. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… maybe about eight months, I think. It sort of started building after I took over the STEM-Arts curriculum initiative. I kept telling myself it was just stress from that, you know, like anyone would feel stretched, but it hasn’t really lifted.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel this started because of the curriculum initiative taking over your life?\n\nPatient: I think it probably contributed, yes. I don’t want to blame it entirely, because I know plenty of people handle big projects and don’t… fall apart or anything. But it does seem like that’s when it started building—around when I took over the STEM-Arts curriculum initiative, about eight months ago. At first I kept telling myself it was just being busy, but it’s sort of stayed with me.\n\nDoctor: If the curriculum initiative were no longer on your plate, do you think you would still feel this way?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not completely sure, but I think some of it would still be there. I’d like to say it’s just the initiative, because that would make sense, wouldn’t it? But even when I’m not actively dealing with it, most days there’s still this heavy gray feeling over everything. I can be in the classroom or at home with my husband and still feel kind of hollow… close to tears, even when nothing specific is happening.\n\nDoctor: Before these past eight months, was there ever a period of at least two months when your mood felt mostly back to normal?\n\nPatient: Before that, yes, I think my mood was mostly normal. I mean, I had ordinary stress, like anyone, but this particular feeling has really just been building for about eight months, since I took over the STEM-Arts curriculum initiative. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about a lot of different things?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say “a lot of different things,” exactly. I mean, I worry, probably more than I should sometimes, but it’s mostly around the school responsibilities and whether I’m keeping up. I’m not sure if that counts as excessive… does it?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your sleep?\n\nPatient: Yes… my sleep hasn’t been great. I mean, I can still function, but it’s been pretty all over the place for months. I’ll lie there replaying grant details or things I need to fix, and then a lot of nights I wake up around three or four and can’t really settle again. Even if I do sleep longer, I wake up feeling like I didn’t actually rest. Is that the kind of change you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually tired or low on energy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I do get tired, but I’ve been telling myself that’s probably just normal with work and not sleeping perfectly. I wouldn’t want to overstate it. Some days I feel a bit drained, but I can still get through what I need to do. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling hopeless?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if hopeless is the right word. I try not to think of it that way. I don’t want to blame it entirely, because I know plenty of people handle big projects and don’t… fall apart or anything. But it does seem like that’s when it started building—around when I took over the STEM-Arts curriculum initiative, about eight months ago. At first I kept telling myself it was just being busy, but it’s sort of stayed with me.\n\nDoctor: If the curriculum initiative were no longer on your plate, do you think you would still feel this way?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not completely sure, but I think some of it would still be there. I’d like to say it’s just the initiative, because that would make sense, wouldn’t it? But even when I’m not actively dealing with it, most days there’s still this heavy gray feeling over everything. I can be in the classroom or at home with my husband and still feel kind of hollow… close to tears, even when nothing specific is happening.\n\nDoctor: Before these past eight months, was there ever a period of at least two months when your mood felt mostly back to normal?\n\nPatient: Before that, yes, I think my mood was mostly normal. I mean, I had ordinary stress, like anyone, but this particular feeling has really just been building for about eight months, since I took over the STEM-Arts curriculum initiative. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about a lot of different things?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say “a lot of different things,” exactly. I mean, I worry, probably more than I should sometimes, but it’s mostly around the school responsibilities and whether I’m keeping up. I’m not sure if that counts as excessive… does it?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your sleep?\n\nPatient: Yes… my sleep hasn’t been great. I mean, I can still function, but it’s been pretty all over the place for months. I’ll lie there replaying grant details or things I need to fix, and then a lot of nights I wake up around three or four and can’t really settle again. Even if I do sleep longer, I wake up feeling like I didn’t actually rest. Is that the kind of change you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually tired or low on energy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I do get tired, but I’ve been telling myself that’s probably just normal with work and not sleeping perfectly. I wouldn’t want to overstate it. Some days I feel a bit drained, but I can still get through what I need to do. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling hopeless?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if hopeless is the right word. I try not to think of it that way. But most days it does feel like there’s this heavy gray film over everything, and I can be in class with students or sitting at home with my husband and still feel… hollow, I guess. I don’t want to sound dramatic, because I know everyone makes mistakes, but when anything goes wrong with the curriculum project—even something small—I start thinking I’m letting the students down. And then I get stuck on this idea that I’m wasting what my parents sacrificed for me to have opportunities like this. I know that may not be totally fair, but it feels pretty real in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I haven’t noticed anything that feels dramatically different. Sometimes I have to reread something or refocus myself, but I think that’s fairly normal, isn’t it? I can still follow things for the most part.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that life isn’t worth living, or thoughts of wanting to die?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t want you to think I’m being unsafe or anything. I can be in the classroom or at home with my husband and still feel kind of hollow… close to tears, even when nothing specific is happening.\n\nDoctor: Before these past eight months, was there ever a period of at least two months when your mood felt mostly back to normal?\n\nPatient: Before that, yes, I think my mood was mostly normal. I mean, I had ordinary stress, like anyone, but this particular feeling has really just been building for about eight months, since I took over the STEM-Arts curriculum initiative. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about a lot of different things?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say “a lot of different things,” exactly. I mean, I worry, probably more than I should sometimes, but it’s mostly around the school responsibilities and whether I’m keeping up. I’m not sure if that counts as excessive… does it?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your sleep?\n\nPatient: Yes… my sleep hasn’t been great. I mean, I can still function, but it’s been pretty all over the place for months. I’ll lie there replaying grant details or things I need to fix, and then a lot of nights I wake up around three or four and can’t really settle again. Even if I do sleep longer, I wake up feeling like I didn’t actually rest. Is that the kind of change you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually tired or low on energy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I do get tired, but I’ve been telling myself that’s probably just normal with work and not sleeping perfectly. I wouldn’t want to overstate it. Some days I feel a bit drained, but I can still get through what I need to do. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling hopeless?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if hopeless is the right word. I try not to think of it that way. But most days it does feel like there’s this heavy gray film over everything, and I can be in class with students or sitting at home with my husband and still feel… hollow, I guess. Like I’m close to tears for no clear reason. Is that what you mean by hopeless?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling especially worthless or guilty lately?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes, a bit. I don’t want to sound dramatic, because I know everyone makes mistakes, but when anything goes wrong with the curriculum project—even something small—I start thinking I’m letting the students down. And then I get stuck on this idea that I’m wasting what my parents sacrificed for me to have opportunities like this. I know that may not be totally fair, but it feels pretty real in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I haven’t noticed anything that feels dramatically different. Sometimes I have to reread something or refocus myself, but I think that’s fairly normal, isn’t it? I can still follow things for the most part.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that life isn’t worth living, or thoughts of wanting to die?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t want you to think I’m being unsafe or anything. I haven’t made a plan. But yes, sometimes, especially late at night when I’m still in the studio, I catch myself thinking it would be a relief if I just… didn’t wake up.\n\nThat scares me to say. It scared me enough that I told my husband I couldn’t keep handling those thoughts alone. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your appetite?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a way I’ve really paid attention to. I mean, some days I’m not that interested in food, but I can’t tell if that’s just being busy or normal stress. I wouldn’t want to overstate it. Should I be tracking that more carefully?\n\nDoctor: If the curriculum initiative were suddenly resolved tomorrow, do you think your low mood would mostly go away?\n\nPatient: I wish I could say yes, because that would make it feel more… solvable. And I’m sure it would help some.\n\nBut I don’t think it’s only that. Most days it’s like there’s this heavy gray film over everything. I can be in the classroom with students, or at home with my husband, and still feel kind of hollow, like I’m right on the edge of crying. I don’t know if that answers what you mean.\n\nDoctor: During these past eight months, has there been a period of at least two weeks when you felt depressed or unable to enjoy things nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is, but it’s been most days for quite a while. There are stretches where everything just has this heavy gray film over it.\n\nEven if I’m in the classroom with students, or at home with my husband, I can still feel really hollow and like I’m close to tears. So… yes, I guess that would be nearly every day for at least a couple weeks.\n\nDoctor: Before these past eight months, have you ever had another period of at least two weeks when you felt depressed or lost interest in things nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I’ve had normal sad periods before, I think, like anyone does, but I don’t know that I’d say it was nearly every day for two weeks or more.\n\nI don’t want to give you the wrong answer… nothing is coming to mind that feels clearly like this did before these past months.\n\nDoctor: Which has felt more prominent for you lately: feeling depressed and unable to enjoy things, or feeling anxious and stressed?\n\nPatient: Probably more the depressed part… I mean, I do get stressed, but the thing that feels bigger is that most days everything has this heavy gray film over it.\n\nI can be in a classroom full of students, or just at home with my husband, and still feel kind of hollow and close to tears. I’ve had rough times before, of course, but I wouldn’t want to call it that unless I was sure.\n\nNothing before these past eight months really stands out to me as being nearly every day for two weeks like this.
